{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}}\cong P_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathbb{H}}^{{s-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\oplus(1-P_{\mathcal{C}}){\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}).

While the Calderon projection is in general hard to compute, we shall formulate a condition on a boundary decomposing projection in order for it to implement higher semiregularity properties. For
s\geq 0
, we introduce the notation

\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D):=P_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathbb{H}}^{{s-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\oplus(1-P_{\mathcal{C}}){\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\cong{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max,s})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm min,s})}$}}},

where the last isomorphism is implemented by
\gamma
by Theorem
6.1
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
s\geq 0
a positive number. We say that a projection
\mathcal{P}_{+}
is
s
-boundary decomposing for
D
if
\mathcal{P}_{+}
is boundary decomposing for
D
and additionally satisfies the following conditions

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{+}:{\mathbb{H}}^{s+\alpha}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{s+\alpha}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a bounded projection for
\alpha\in\left\{-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}\right\}
,

(2)

\mathcal{P}_{+}:\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)\to\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)
, and
\mathcal{P}_{-}:=(I-\mathcal{P}_{+}):\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)\to{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
, and

(3)

\|u\|_{\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)}\simeq\|\mathcal{P}_{-}u\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})}+\|\mathcal{P}_{+}u\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s-{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})}.

Similarly to Theorem
3.30
and Corollary
3.32
, we have the following result providing a sufficient condition for a projection being
s
-boundary decomposing.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
,
s\geq 0
a positive number and
P
a continuous projection on
{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Assume that
P
satisfies the following:

•

P
is continuous also in the norms on
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
,
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)
,
{\mathbb{H}}^{{-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
,
{\mathbb{H}}^{{s-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

•

The operator
A:=P_{\mathcal{C}}-(1-P)
defines a Fredholm operator on
{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and by continuity extends to Fredholm operators on
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
,
\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)
and
{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Then
P
is
s
-boundary decomposing, and in particular

\|u\|_{\check{\mathrm{H}}^{s}(D)}\simeq\|(1-P)u\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})}+\|Pu\|_{{\mathbb{H}}^{s-{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})}.

In the special case that
P\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a projection such that

•

[P_{\mathcal{C}},P]\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{-m}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
; and

•

the operator
A:=P_{\mathcal{C}}-(1-P)\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is elliptic,

then
P
is
s
-boundary decomposing.

Let us consider a corollary of Theorem
6.1
.

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
s\geq 0
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max})\cap

\displaystyle{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max}):D_{\rm max}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F),\;P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma u\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\}.

Proof.

The inclusion
\subseteq
is clear, we need to prove that if
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max})
satisfies that
D_{\rm max}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
and
P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma u\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
then
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. After replacing
u
with
u-\mathcal{K}_{s+m}P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma u
we can assume that
P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma u=0
. In other words, we need to prove that if
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})
and
D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
then
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
.

Define
\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s}:=(1-P_{\mathcal{C}}){\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and consider the associated realisation
D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s}
of
D
on
{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
. We note that
D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s}
is semi-regular in the sense that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
.

Theorem
6.1
implies that
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\cap\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})
is a core for
D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s}
for any
s
. Therefore, we have dense inclusions

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}({\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\cap\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}))

\displaystyle\subset\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}({\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\cap\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}))

\displaystyle\subset\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})\cap{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E).

However, the spaces
\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})=\mathrm{ran}(D_{\max,s})=D{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
and
\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})\cap{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
are closed in
{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
, and therefore,
\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})=\mathrm{ran}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})\cap{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
.
The condition that
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}})
with
D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
and
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
therefore implies that there exists
v\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
such that
Dv=D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}}u
.
We then have that
D(v-u)=0
, so
\gamma(v-u)\in\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{C}^{c}=0
, and therefore
v-u\in\ker(D_{\min})\subset{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)
.
Hence,
u=v+(u-v)\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)+{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)={\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
which proves the required claim.
∎

6.2.
Higher order regularity of boundary conditions

In
[
7
,
8
]
, higher order regularity of boundary conditions was introduced under the name of regular boundary conditions. The regularity was stated in terms of graphical decompositions of regular boundary conditions. We shall formulate a slightly more general condition.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator,
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
a semi-regular boundary condition (in
{\rm L}^{2}
-sense),
s\geq 0
and
P
an
s
-boundary decomposing projection.

•

We say that
B
is
s
-semiregular (w.r.t
P
) if for any
\xi\in B
it holds that
\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
if and only if
(1-P)\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

•

We say that the realisation
D_{B}
is
s
-semiregular if whenever
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})
satisfies that
D_{\rm B}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
then
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
.

The reader should note that the proof of Corollary
6.4
essentially boils down to proving that
\mathcal{C}^{c}
is
s
-semiregular for any
s\geq 0
(w.r.t
P_{\mathcal{C}}
).
Therefore, Corollary
6.4
partially proves the main result of this section (Theorem
6.6
below) in the special case of the boundary condition
\mathcal{C}^{c}
.

Theorem 6.6

(Higher order boundary regularity)
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
,
s\geq 0
and
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
a boundary condition. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

D_{B}
is
s
-semiregular.

(2)

B
is
s
-semiregular with respect to
P_{\mathcal{C}}

(3)

B
is
s
-semiregular with respect to any
s
-boundary decomposing projection.

Proof.

We start by showing that if
P
is an
s
-boundary decomposing projection and there is a
\xi\in B
with
(1-P)\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
but
\xi\notin{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
, then there is a
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})
satisfying that
D_{\rm B}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
and
u\notin{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. Indeed, we take a preimage
u\in\gamma^{-1}(\xi)
written as
u=u_{0}+u_{1}\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max,s})
where
\gamma(u_{0})=(1-P)\xi
and
\gamma(u_{1})=P\xi
. Since
(1-P)\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
we can assume that
u_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. However, if
(1-P)\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
but
\xi\notin{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
then
P\xi\notin{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
so
u_{1}\notin{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. This proves that 1) implies 3).

It is clear that 3) implies 2). It remains to prove that 2) implies 1), i.e that if
B
is
s
-semiregular with respect to
P_{\mathcal{C}}
then whenever
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})
satisfies that
D_{\rm B}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
then
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. Take a
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})
satisfying that
D_{\rm B}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F)
. By applying the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
6.4
, we have that
w:=u-\mathcal{K}\gamma u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\mathcal{C}^{c}_{s},s})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. On the other hand
\gamma(w)=\gamma(u)-P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma(u)=(1-P_{\mathcal{C}})\gamma(u)\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
so by weak
s
-semiregularity
\gamma(u)\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Therefore
P_{\mathcal{C}}\gamma(u)\in{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(M;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and can conclude that
\mathcal{K}\gamma u=\mathcal{K}_{s+m}\gamma u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
by Theorem
6.1
. We conclude that
u=w+\mathcal{K}\gamma u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
. We conclude that 1) and 2) are equivalent.
∎

Based on the graphical decompositions of Section
5
, let us provide a method of producing semiregular boundary conditions.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
B
be a boundary condition for an elliptic differential operator
D
of order
m>0
,
s\geq 0
and
P
an
s
-boundary decomposing projection. Assume that
B
is graphically decomposable with respect to
P
as in Definition
5.3
. Then
B
is
s
-semiregular if and only if

\displaystyle W_{+}

\displaystyle\subseteq{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle g(V_{+}

\displaystyle\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}))\subseteq V_{-}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{{s+m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}).

The proof goes ad verbatim to
[
8
, Lemma 8.9]
.

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
a boundary condition. Then the realisation
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
of
D^{\dagger}D
is regular if
B
is regular and
m
-semiregular.

Proof.

Before getting down to brass tacks, we note that the operator
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
is self-adjoint, so
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
is a regular realisation if and only if it is a semi-regular realisation. If
B
is regular and
m
-semiregular then any
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B})
satisfies
D_{\rm B}u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B}^{*})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;F)
, because
B
is regular. Since
B
is
m
-semiregular, Theorem
6.6
implies that
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm 2m}(M;E)
when
D_{\rm B}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;F)
. We conclude that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm 2m}(M;E)
if
B
is regular and
m
-semiregular.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

We note that an interpolation argument shows that if
D
is an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
a boundary condition such that
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
is regular, then
B
is semi-regular. Since the proof of Corollary
6.8
only used semi-regularity of
B^{*}
and
m
-semiregularity of
B
, we conclude that any
m
-semiregular boundary condition
B
with
B^{*}
semi-regular is regular.

6.3.
Higher regularity of the Dirichlet problem

As an example of our theory, we consider elliptic operators with a uniquely solvable Dirichlet problem. Such differential operators play a prominent role in
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
. The results of this subsection are well known. Along the way, we shall exemplify the results of this paper for operators with positive principal symbols.

For an elliptic differential operator
D
of even order
m
, we define the Dirichlet condition as the space

B_{\rm Dir}:=\{\xi=(\xi_{j})_{j=0}^{m-1}\in\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{1}{2}-m}(\Sigma;E\otimes C^{m}):\xi_{j}=0,\;j=0,\ldots,\frac{m}{2}-1\}.

We say that
D
has a uniquely solvable Dirichlet problem if
B_{\rm Dir}
is a regular boundary condition and
D_{\rm Dir}:=D_{B_{\rm Dir}}
is an invertible realisation. We note the following important example.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
D=D^{\dagger}
be a formally selfadjoint elliptic operator of order
m
acting on the sections of a hermitian vector bundle
E
on an
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary. Assume that
D
has positive interior principal symbol
\sigma_{D}
. Then
D_{\rm Dir}
is the realisation with domain
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm Dir}):={\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)\cap{\rm H}^{\rm m/2}_{\rm 0}(M;E)
. Moreover,
D_{\rm Dir}
is regular, self-adjoint and lower semibounded. In particular, for large enough
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
D+\lambda
has a uniquely solvable Dirichlet problem.

We remark that if
D
has positive interior symbol, the symmetry property
\sigma_{D}(x,-\xi)=(-1)^{m}\sigma_{D}(x,\xi)
implies that
m
is even.

Proof.

By extending
D
to a self-adjoint elliptic differential operator with positive principal symbol on a closed manifold containing
M
as a smooth subdomain, we see that the Gårding inequality implies that for
\lambda>0
large enough it holds that

\langle(D+\lambda)\varphi,\varphi\rangle_{{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)}\sim\|\varphi\|_{{\rm H}^{\rm m/2}_{\rm 0}(M;E)}^{2},

for
\varphi\in{\rm C}^{\infty}_{\rm c}(M;E)
. Therefore
D_{c}
is lower semi-bounded and symmetric, and
D_{\rm Dir}
is indeed the Friedrichs extension of
D_{c}
. Therefore,
D_{\rm Dir}
is self-adjoint and lower semibounded. Since
D_{\rm Dir}
is semi-regular by construction, self-adjointness implies regularity.
∎

We now describe a certain projection used in
[
35
]
. By Lemma
6.10
unique solvability of the Dirichlet problem is no serious restriction for operators with positive principal symbol. Under this assumption,
\gamma
restricts to an isomorphism
\ker(D_{\rm max})\cong\mathcal{C}_{D}
. Since
D_{\rm Dir}
is invertible and regular, the operator

P_{\zeta}:=1-D_{\rm Dir}^{-1}D_{\rm max},

is a continuous projection on
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
. The projection
P_{\zeta}
is the projection onto
\ker(D_{\rm max})
along
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm Dir})
. As such,
P_{\zeta}
defines a Banach space isomorphism

(1-P_{\zeta})\oplus P_{\zeta}:\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm Dir})\oplus\ker(D_{\rm max}).

For more details, see
[
35
, Chapter II]
. Under
\gamma
, we arrive at a decomposition

\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)=B_{\rm Dir}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{D},

as Banach spaces when topologising
B_{\rm Dir}
as a subspace of
{\mathbb{H}}^{m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
. We let
P_{\zeta,\partial}
denote the continuous projection in
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
onto
\mathcal{C}_{D}
along
B_{\rm Dir}
; note that
P_{\zeta,\partial}
is induced from
P_{\zeta}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator of even order with uniquely solvable Dirichlet problem. Let
s\geq 0
. The projection
P_{\zeta,\partial}
is
s
-boundary decomposing for
D
and is of order zero in the Douglis-Nirenberg calculus
P_{\zeta,\partial}\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Proof.

We write
\gamma=(\gamma_{D},\gamma_{N})
where

\displaystyle\gamma_{D}:

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m/2}),\quad u\mapsto(\partial_{x_{n}}^{k}u|_{\Sigma})_{k=0}^{m/2-1},\mbox{ and}

\displaystyle\gamma_{N}:

\displaystyle\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{-m/2-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m/2}),\quad u\mapsto(\partial_{x_{n}}^{k}u|_{\Sigma})_{k=m/2}^{m-1}.

By
[
36
]
, there is a Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\Lambda_{DN}\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{m}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m/2})
defined from
\Lambda_{DN}\xi:=\gamma_{N}u
where
u\in\ker(D_{\max})
satisfies
\gamma_{D}u=\xi
. A short computation shows that

P_{\zeta,\partial}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\Lambda_{DN}&0\end{pmatrix},

(23)

in the decomposition

\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\subseteq{\mathbb{H}}^{-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})={\mathbb{H}}^{-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m/2})\oplus{\mathbb{H}}^{-m/2-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m/2}).

It follows that
P_{\zeta,\partial}\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

By construction,
1-P_{\zeta,\partial}
maps
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
into
B_{\rm Dir}\subseteq{\mathbb{H}}^{m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and defines a decomposition
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)=B_{\rm Dir}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{D+\lambda}
of Banach spaces. We conclude that
P_{\zeta,\partial}
is boundary decomposing for
D
. The fact that
P_{\zeta,\partial}
is
s
-boundary decomposing for all
s\geq 0
follows from Theorem
6.3
.
∎

Example 6.12
.

An immediate consequence of Lemma
6.11
and Theorem
6.6
is the well known fact that the Dirichlet problem is
s
-semiregular for all
s\geq 0
if it is uniquely solvable in
{\rm L}^{2}
. With respect to
P_{\zeta,\partial}
, we can graphically decompose the Dirichlet condition as

B_{\rm Dir}=\left\{v+gv:v\in V_{-}\right\}\oplus W_{+},

for

\displaystyle V_{-}=

\displaystyle(1-P_{\zeta,\partial})\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)=(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}){\mathbb{H}}^{m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\displaystyle V_{+}=

\displaystyle P_{\zeta,\partial}\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)=P_{\zeta,\partial}{\mathbb{H}}^{m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\displaystyle g=

\displaystyle 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad W_{+}=W_{-}=0.

Example 6.13
.

Another consequence of Lemma
6.11
and Theorem
6.6
concerns Robin-type problems with claims concerning
s
-semiregularity for all
s\geq 0
if the Dirichlet problem is uniquely solvable in
{\rm L}^{2}
. This is studied in more detail and generality in
[
37
]
.

We give an example in the case that
D
is second order with positive principal symbol, e.g. a Laplace type operator. For an element
a\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathrm{End}(E))
, consider the boundary condition

B_{a}:=\{(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\in\check{\mathrm{H}}(D):a\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}=0\}.

This boundary condition corresponds to the Robin problem for
D
, i.e. for
f\in{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)
, the equation
D_{B_{a}}u=f
is equivalent to the Robin problem

\begin{cases}Du=f,\quad&\mbox{in $M$}\\
au|_{\Sigma}+\partial_{n}u|_{\Sigma}=0,\quad&\mbox{on $\Sigma$}.\end{cases}

The boundary condition
B_{a}
is a pseudolocal boundary condition
B_{a}=B_{P_{a}}
where

P_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
a&1\end{pmatrix}.

A computation with Proposition
2.26
(cf.
[
37
]
) shows that
B_{a}
is regular.

We compute from Equation (
23
) that

(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda))\xi=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\Lambda_{DN}\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

(24)

Take an
s\geq 0
, and assume that
\xi\in B_{a}
satisfies
(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda))\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{s+m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
, or equivalently (by Equation (
24
)) that
-\Lambda_{DN}\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m-3/2}(\Sigma;E)
. If
(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\in B_{a}
, we therefore have that
(\Lambda_{DN}+a)\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m-3/2}(\Sigma;E)
. Since
\Lambda_{DN}\in\Psi^{1}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E)
is elliptic, we conclude from elliptic regularity that if
\xi=(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\in B_{a}
satisfies
(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda))\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{s+m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
then
\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m-1/2}(\Sigma;E)
so
\xi\in{\mathbb{H}}^{s+m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
. We conclude that
B_{a}
is
s
-semiregular for any
s\geq 0
when
a\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathrm{End}(E))
. In particular, if
f\in{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;E)
, any solution
u\in{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)
to the Robin problem

\begin{cases}Du=f,\quad&\mbox{in $M$}\\
au|_{\Sigma}+\partial_{n}u|_{\Sigma}=0,\quad&\mbox{on $\Sigma$},\end{cases}

satisfies
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(M;E)
.

Let us graphically decompose the Robin condition
B_{a}
with respect to
P_{\zeta,\partial}
. For
\xi=(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})=(\xi_{0},-a\xi_{0})\in B_{a}
, we can write

\xi=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{0}\\
\Lambda_{DN}\xi_{0}\end{pmatrix}}_{\in P_{\zeta,\partial}\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\Lambda_{DN}\xi_{0}-a\xi_{0}\end{pmatrix}}_{\in(1-P_{\zeta,\partial})\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}.

The operator
\Lambda_{DN}
is first order elliptic, and so is
\Lambda_{DN}+a
. So there exists an elliptic
T\in\Psi^{-1}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E)
such that
1-(\Lambda_{DN}+a)T
and
1-T(\Lambda_{DN}+a)
are smoothing finite rank projectors,
\mathrm{ran}((\Lambda_{DN}+a)T)=\mathrm{ran}(\Lambda_{DN}+a)
and
\ker(T(\Lambda_{DN}+a))=\ker(\Lambda_{DN}+a)
. Define the space

V_{-}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\eta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\in(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}){\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}):(\Lambda_{DN}+a)T\eta_{1}=\eta_{1}\right\}.

The space

W_{-}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\eta_{1}\end{pmatrix}\in(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}){\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}):(\Lambda_{DN}+a)T\eta_{1}=0\right\},

is by the construction of
T
a finite-dimensional subspace of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
such that

V_{-}\oplus W_{-}=(1-P_{\zeta,\partial}){\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Define the space

V_{+}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{0}\\
\Lambda_{DN}\eta_{0}\end{pmatrix}\in P_{\zeta,\partial}{\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}):T(\Lambda_{DN}+a)\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}\right\}.

The space

W_{+}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{0}\\
\Lambda_{DN}\eta_{0}\end{pmatrix}\in P_{\zeta,\partial}{\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}):T(\Lambda_{DN}+a)\eta_{0}=0\right\},

is by the construction of
T
a finite-dimensional subspace of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
such that

V_{+}\oplus W_{+}=P_{\zeta,\partial}{\mathbb{H}}^{3/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}).

Define the map
g:V_{-}\to V_{+}
by

g\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\eta_{1}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}-T\eta_{1}\\
-\Lambda_{DN}T\eta_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

A short computation shows that

{g}^{\ast}({V}^{\ast}_{+}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\rm op}))\subset{V}^{\ast}_{-}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{{{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}_{\rm op}),

and that

B_{a}=\left\{v+gv:v\in V_{-}\right\}\oplus W_{+}.

This gives a graphical decomposition of
B_{a}
. The existence of a graphical decomposition will by Theorem
5.6
give an alternative proof of the fact that
B_{a}
is elliptic. The construction of
g
in terms of a pseudodifferential operator, combined with Proposition
6.7
, gives an alternative explanation for the fact that
B_{a}
is
s
-semiregular for any
s\geq 0
.

7.
Differential operators with positive principal symbols and their Weyl laws

In this section we shall prove a Weyl law for general lower semi-bounded self-adjoint regular realisations of an elliptic differential operator
D
of order
m>0
with positive interior principal symbol. Weyl laws with error estimates are known under mild assumptions from
[
2
,
49
]
, see also
[
39
, Theorem 1]
. The authors are grateful to Gerd Grubb who showed us how to prove the Weyl law for general boundary conditions by combining the spectral asymptotics of the Dirichlet realisation with
[
35
]
.

The Weyl formula for the asymptotic behaviour of the eigenvalues of Dirichlet realisations of elliptic operators dates back to Weyl
[
71
]
, where his focus was on the Laplacian. Weyl’s result was extended to more general operators in many contributions to the literature, as summed up e.g. in Agmon’s paper
[
2
]
(including the treatment by Gårding
[
29
]
of variable-coefficient operators). In
[
2
]
and subsequent treatments by Hörmander
[
49
]
, Duistermaat-Guillemin
[
24
]
, Seeley
[
65
]
and Ivrii
[
52
]
the focus has been on the remainder term. See more in the survey of Ivrii
[
53
]
. We shall here just study the principal asymptotics, however for realisations that are more general than those defined by pseudodifferential boundary conditions.

We now use results of
[
35
,
43
]
to describe the resolvent of
D_{\rm B}
under the assumptions stated above. The method of proof will be to reduce the problem to a well behaved boundary condition by studying the resolvent difference

(D_{\rm B}+\lambda)^{-1}-(D_{\rm Dir}+\lambda)^{-1},

for the Dirichlet extension
D_{\rm Dir}
. The idea of studying this resolvent difference and its localisation to the boundary is due to Birman
[
11
,
12
]
. The precise description that we use follows Grubb
[
35
]
. Recall the results of Subsection
6.3
for the Dirichlet realisation. We write
P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)
for the boundary decomposing projection on
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
induced by the projection
P_{\zeta}(\lambda):=1-(D_{\rm Dir}+\lambda)^{-1}(D_{\rm max}+\lambda)
on
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max})
(cf. Lemma
6.11
).

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
D=D^{\dagger}
be formally selfadjoint elliptic operator of order
m
acting on the sections of a hermitian vector bundle
E
on an
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary. Assume that
D
has positive interior principal symbol
\sigma_{D}
and that
B
is a regular and self-adjoint boundary condition. Then
D_{\rm B}
has discrete spectrum and for some
\lambda>0
large enough then we can write

(D_{\rm B}+\lambda)^{-1}=Q_{+}+G+\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B},

where

(1)

Q_{+}
is the truncation of a pseudo-differential operator
\hat{Q}\in\Psi^{-m}_{\rm cl}(\hat{M};\hat{E})
that acts on a hermitian vector bundle
\hat{E}
extending
E
to a closed manifold
\hat{M}
containing
M
as a smooth domain, and satisfies that

\sigma_{-m}(\hat{Q})|_{T^{*}M\setminus M}=\sigma_{D}^{-1}.

(2)

G
is a singular Green operator of order
-m
and class
0
(cf.
[
43
]
).

(3)

\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B}
is an operator that factors over continuous mappings

\displaystyle{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)\xrightarrow{P_{\ker(D_{\rm max}+\lambda)}}

\displaystyle\ker(D_{\rm max}+\lambda)\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{D+\lambda}\xrightarrow{P_{\overline{P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B}^{\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}}}\overline{P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B}^{\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{T}_{\rm B}^{-1}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{T}_{\rm B}^{-1}}P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\rm L}^{2}(M;E),

where
P_{\ker(D_{\rm max}+\lambda)}
and
P_{\overline{P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B}^{\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}}
denotes the orthogonal projectors and
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm B}:P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B\to\overline{P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B}^{\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}
is the operator constructed in
[
35
, Chapter II]
(and there denoted by the densely defined
T:V\to W
for
V=W=\overline{P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B}^{\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)}
with
\mathrm{dom}(T)=P_{\zeta,\partial}(\lambda)B
).

We remark that all three operators
Q_{+}
,
G
and
\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B}
depend on the choice of
\lambda
. However,
Q_{+}
and
G
are independent of the boundary condition
B
(a priori there is an implicit dependence via the choice of
\lambda
, but it is irrelevant for the structural statements we are interested in).

Proof.

It follows from
[
43
, Section 3.3]
that we, for
\lambda>0
outside a discrete set of real numbers, can write

(D_{\rm Dir}+\lambda)^{-1}=Q_{+}+G,

where
Q_{+}
and
G
are as in the statement of the theorem. The operator
D_{\rm B}
is self-adjoint because
D
is formally self-adjoint and
B
is Lagrangian. Since
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)
, the inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm B})\hookrightarrow{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)
is compact. Therefore
D_{\rm B}
has only discrete spectrum. For
\lambda
outside a discrete subset so that
D_{\rm B}+\lambda
and
D_{\rm Dir}+\lambda
are invertible, it follows from
[
35
, Chapter II, Theorem 1.4]
that

(D_{\rm B}+\lambda)^{-1}-(D_{\rm Dir}+\lambda)^{-1}=\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B},

where
\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B}
is as in the statement of the theorem. This proves the theorem.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

The singular values of the operators appearing in Theorem
7.1
have the following asymptotics:

(1)

\mu_{k}(Q_{+})=c_{Q}k^{-\frac{m}{n}}+o(k^{-\frac{m}{n}})
where

c_{Q}=\left(\frac{1}{n(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{S^{*}M}\mathrm{Tr}_{E}(\sigma_{m}(Q)(x,\xi)^{\frac{n}{m}})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{\frac{m}{n}}.

(2)

\mu_{k}(G)=O(k^{-\frac{m}{n-1}})

(3)

\mu_{k}(\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B})=O(k^{-\frac{m}{n-1}})

Proof.

Item i) follows from
[
43
, Proposition 4.5.3]
. One can also prove item i) by hand by applying the Weyl law on a closed manifold, see e.g.
[
51
, Chapter XXIX]
or
[
67
, Chapter III]
, to suitable perturbations of
G_{+}
. The statement of item ii) follows from
[
41
]
. The statement of item iii) follows from that
\mathfrak{G}_{\rm B}
factors over the inclusion
{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
whose singular value sequence is
O(k^{-m/(n-1)})
as
k\to\infty
. Indeed, using a Weyl law on the
n-1
-dimensional compact manifold
\Sigma
shows that the singular values of
{\rm H}^{\rm s+m}(\Sigma;E)\hookrightarrow{\rm H}^{\rm s}(\Sigma;E)
is
O(k^{-m/(n-1)})
as
k\to\infty
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

The statement that
\mu_{k}(Q_{+}+G)=\mu_{k}(D_{\rm Dir}^{-1})=c_{Q}k^{-\frac{m}{n}}+o(k^{-\frac{m}{n}})
is a direct consequence of the spectral asymptotics of the Dirichlet realisation, see
[
2
]
. Compare to
[
29
]
and
[
39
, Theorem 1]
. Therefore
\mu_{k}(Q_{+})=c_{Q}k^{-\frac{m}{n}}+o(k^{-\frac{m}{n}})
follows from that
\mu_{k}(G)=o(k^{-\frac{m}{n}})
by
[
41
]
and the Weyl-Fan inequality (see for instance
[
68
, Theorem 1.7]
). The proof above is included since it shows how the different components in the resolvent of
D_{B}
contributes to the spectral asymptotics.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
D=D^{\dagger}
be formally selfadjoint elliptic operator of order
m
acting on the sections of a hermitian vector bundle
E
on an
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary. Assume that
D
has positive interior principal symbol
\sigma_{D}
and that
B
is a regular and self-adjoint boundary condition defining a lower semi-bounded realisation
D_{B}
. Define the constant

c_{D}=\left(\frac{1}{n(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{S^{*}M}\mathrm{Tr}_{E}(\sigma_{D}(x,\xi)^{-n/m})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{-m/n}.

The operator
D_{\rm B}
has discrete real spectrum, and its spectrum
\lambda_{0}(D_{\rm B})\leq\lambda_{1}(D_{\rm B})\leq\lambda_{2}(D_{\rm B})\leq\cdots
satisfies that

\lambda_{k}(D_{\rm B})=c_{D}k^{m/n}+o(k^{\frac{m}{n}}),\quad\mbox{as $k\to+\infty$}.

Proof.

Using Theorem
7.1
, Lemma
7.2
and the Weyl-Fan inequality (see for instance
[
68
, Theorem 1.7]
) we conclude that

\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B})=c_{D}k^{m/n}+o(k^{\frac{m}{n}}),\quad\mbox{as $k\to+\infty$}.

Here
(\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
denotes the singular value sequence of the unbounded operator
D_{B}
, so by definition
\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B})=\sqrt{\mu_{k}((1+D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B})^{-1})^{-1}-1}
. Since
D_{B}
is self-adjoint and lower semi-bounded,
\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B})=\lambda_{k}(D_{\rm B})
for
k
large enough and the theorem follows.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

The operator
D_{B}
is lower semi-bounded if and only if the operator
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm B}
from Theorem
7.1
, item iii), is lower semi-bounded. For a certain class of elliptic boundary conditions, the operator
\mathfrak{T}_{\rm B}
is realised as a pseudodifferential operator in
[
37
, Section 8]
and in this case the asymptotics of the negative eigenvalues was studied.

Remark 7.6
.

Theorem
7.4
was proven in
[
2
]
under the additional hypothesis that there is a
k>n/m
such that
D_{B}^{k}
is regular, i.e.
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B}^{k})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm km}(M;E)
. By complex interpolation, using that
D_{B}
is self-adjoint, this condition implies
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B}^{l})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm lm}(M;E)
for
l=1,\ldots,k
. Note that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B}^{k})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm km}(M;E)
if and only if
B
is
m(k-1)
-semiregular. Let us provide a simple example where the assumption appearing in
[
2
]
fails, but Theorem
7.4
holds. Our example is quite similar to Example
6.13
and provided solely for the purpose of giving intuition for the difference in assumptions between Theorem
7.4
and
[
2
]
.

Assume that
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact domain with smooth boundary and consider a shift of the standard Laplacian

D=\Delta+1=1-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}.

In particular, both the Dirichlet problem and the Neumann problem for
D
are uniquely solvable. For a real-valued function
a\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega,\mathbb{R})
, consider the Robin boundary condition

B_{a}:=\{(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\in\check{\mathrm{H}}(D):a\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}=0\}.

A short computation with Proposition
2.26
(or in other words using Green’s formula) shows that
B_{a}
is self-adjoint. Since the operator
D_{B_{a}}
is defined from the closed lower semibounded quadratic form

q_{a}(u):=\int_{M}(|u|^{2}+|\nabla u|^{2})\mathrm{d}V(x)+\int_{\Sigma}a|u|^{2}\mathrm{d}S(x),\quad u\in{\rm H}^{\rm 1}(M),

the operator
D_{B_{a}}
is lower semi-bounded and so satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
7.4
as soon as
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)
, i.e.
B_{a}
is semi-regular (or equivalently regular, by self-adjointness).

By the same argument as in Example
6.13
,
B_{a}
is
s
-semiregular if and only if for any
(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})\in B_{a}
, the property
(\Lambda_{DN}+a)\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+1/2}(\Sigma)
implies that
\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+3/2}(\Sigma)
. If
a
is such that the multiplication operator
a:{\rm H}^{\rm s+3/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm s+1/2}(\Sigma)
is compact, then for a generic
4
4

4

In the sense that for
\varepsilon\in(-1,1)
outside of a discrete set of points
\Lambda_{DN}+a+\varepsilon:{\rm H}^{\rm s+3/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm s+1/2}(\Sigma)
is invertible.
such
a
, the boundary condition
B_{a}
is
s
-semiregular. Conversely, if
B_{a}
is
s
-semi-regular, then elliptic regularity of
\Lambda_{DN}
and the fact that
(\Lambda_{DN}+a)\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+1/2}(\Sigma)\;\Rightarrow\xi_{0}\in{\rm H}^{\rm s+3/2}(\Sigma)
implies that
a:{\rm H}^{\rm s+3/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm s+1/2}(\Sigma)
is continuous.

Let us now give a condition on
a
such that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}}^{k})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm km}(M;E)
holds for
k=1
but not for
k=2
, and therefore for no
k>1
.
We note that
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}}^{2})
if and only if
D_{B_{a}}u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}})
. Therefore, the inclusion
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}}^{2})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm 4}(M)
holds if and only if for
u\in\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}})
such that
D_{B_{a}}u\in{\rm H}^{\rm 2}(M)
it holds that
u\in{\rm H}^{\rm 4}(M)
. From Theorem
6.6
we see that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B_{a}}^{2})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm 4}(M)
if and only if
B_{a}
is
2
-semiregular. By the argument above, this occurs if the function
a
makes the multiplication operator
a:{\rm H}^{\rm 3/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm 1/2}(\Sigma)
compact but the multiplication operator
a:{\rm H}^{\rm 7/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(\Sigma)
is not even continuous. This occurs when
a\in{\rm C}^{1}(\Sigma)
but
a\notin{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(\Sigma)
. Indeed, continuity of
a:{\rm H}^{\rm 7/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(\Sigma)
fails if
a=a1\notin{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm C}^{1}(\Sigma)
acts continuously
{\rm H}^{\rm 1/2}(\Sigma)
(by interpolation of the continuous actions
{\rm H}^{\rm 1}(\Sigma)
and
{\rm L}^{2}(\Sigma)
) and
{\rm H}^{\rm 3/2}(\Sigma)\to{\rm H}^{\rm 1/2}(\Sigma)
is compact by Rellich’s theorem. For an example of an
a\in{\rm C}^{1}(\Sigma)
with
a\notin{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(\Sigma)
, take
M
to be the unit disc, so
\Sigma=S^{1}
, and define
a
from the lacunary Fourier series

a(z):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-2k}(z^{2^{k}}+z^{-2^{k}})\in{\rm C}^{1}(S^{1})\setminus{\rm H}^{\rm 5/2}(S^{1}).

(25)

For
a
as in Equation (
25
), the regular realisation
(1+\Delta)_{B_{a}}
on the unit disc provides a concrete example where the Weyl law holds by Theorem
7.4
but the assumption
\mathrm{dom}(D_{B}^{k})\subseteq{\rm H}^{\rm km}(M;E)
for some
k>n/m
from
[
2
]
fails.

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
D:{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
be an elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
a regular and
m
-semiregular boundary condition. Then the sequence of singular values of the operator
D_{\rm B}:{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)\dashrightarrow{\rm L}^{2}(M;F)
satisfies that

\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B})=\sqrt{c_{D^{\dagger}D}}k^{\frac{m}{n}}+o(k^{\frac{m}{n}}),\quad\mbox{as $k\to+\infty$},

where

c_{D^{\dagger}D}=\left(\frac{1}{n(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{S^{*}M}\mathrm{Tr}_{E}((\sigma_{D}(x,\xi)^{*}\sigma_{D}(x,\xi))^{-n/2m})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{-2m/n},

and
\sigma_{D}
denotes the interior principal symbol of
D
.

Proof.

We have that
\mu_{k}(D_{\rm B})^{2}=\lambda_{k}(D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B})
and by Corollary
6.8
,
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
is regular if
B
is regular and
m
-semiregular. The corollary follows from Theorem
7.4
since
D_{\rm B}^{*}D_{\rm B}
is non-negative.
∎

Remark 7.8
.

A consequence of Corollary
7.7
is that if
D
is a formally self-adjoint, elliptic differential operator of order
m>0
and
B\subseteq\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
is a Lagrangian, regular and
m
-semiregular boundary condition, then
D_{\rm B}
has discrete spectrum consisting of real eigenvalues that asymptotically behaves as

|\lambda_{k}(D_{\rm B})|=c_{|D|}k^{\frac{m}{n}}+o(k^{\frac{m}{n}}),\quad\mbox{as $k\to+\infty$},

where

c_{|D|}=\left(\frac{1}{n(2\pi)^{n}}\int_{S^{*}M}\mathrm{Tr}_{E}(|\sigma_{D}(x,\xi)|^{-n/m})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi\right)^{-m/n}.

For more details on the spectral asymptotics of the sequences of negative and positive eigenvalues, see
[
43
, Chapter 4.5]
.

Remark 7.9
.

The assumption that
B
is regular in Theorem
7.4
can in general not be dropped. To give an example thereof, we assume for simplicity that
D
is as in Theorem
7.4
and
D_{\rm Dir}
is invertible. Following the notations of
[
35
,
37
]
, for a small
m^{\prime}<m
we take a regular
T_{0}\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{m^{\prime}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
with
[T_{0},P_{\mathcal{C}}]\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{m^{\prime}-m}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and define
T:=P_{\mathcal{C}}T_{0}P_{\mathcal{C}}
. The operator
T
is a densely defined Fredholm operator
\mathcal{C}_{D}\to\mathcal{C}_{D}
with
\mathrm{dom}(T)=P_{\mathcal{C}}{\mathbb{H}}^{{m^{\prime}-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Assume for simplicity that
T
is invertible. Let
D_{T}
be the realisation of
D
constructed as in
[
35
, Chapter II, Proposition 1.2]
. The realisation
D_{T}
is not regular, but
\gamma(\mathrm{dom}(D_{T}))\subseteq{\mathbb{H}}^{{m^{\prime}-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
, cf.
[
37
, Corollary 6.10]
. By
[
35
, Chapter II, Theorem 1.4]
, we have that

D_{T}^{-1}-D_{\rm Dir}=T^{-1},

where
T^{-1}
is “extended by zero” to an operator on
{\rm L}^{2}(M;E)=\mathcal{C}_{D}\oplus\mathrm{ran}(D_{\rm min})
. If
m^{\prime}<m(n-1)/n
, the proof of Theorem
7.4
breaks down as it only produces the lower estimate

\mu_{k}(D_{T})\gtrsim k^{\frac{m^{\prime}}{n-1}},\quad\mbox{as $k\to\infty$}.

8.
Rigidity results for the index of elliptic differential operators

In this section we prove a rigidity result for the Fredholm index of Fredholm realisations
D_{\rm B}
. As noted in Remark
2.13
, the index formula of Theorem
2.12
implies homotopy invariance for the choice of Fredholm boundary condition when
D
is fixed. We now extend this observation to more general homotopies.

We topologise the space of pseudodifferential operators by declaring the symbol mappings and its local splittings defined from Kohn-Nirenberg quantisation, as well as the inclusion of the smoothing operators, to be continuous. The space of order
m
differential operators
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
will be topologised as linear operators between the Fréchet topologies of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)
and
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
. Equivalently, we can topologise the space of order
m
differential operators
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
in terms of the
{\rm C}^{\infty}
-topology on the coefficients of the operator.

Theorem 8.1
.

Assume that
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of elliptic differential operators of order
m>0
such that

(1)

The family is continuous in the topology on the space of order
m
differential operators
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
.

(2)

\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,{\rm max}})
is independent of
t
.

Let
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto P_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\check{\mathrm{H}}(D))
be a norm continuous family of projectors such that
B_{t}:=(1-P_{t})\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
satisfies that
(B_{t},\mathcal{C}_{D_{t}})
is a Fredholm pair in
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Then it holds that

\indx(D_{0,B_{0}})=\indx(D_{1,B_{1}}).

In order to prove this theorem, we first prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma 8.2
.

Assume that
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of elliptic differential operators of order
m>0
as in Theorem
8.1
. Then viewing
D_{t,\max}
as a continuous linear mapping
\mathrm{dom}(D_{0,\max})\to{\rm L}^{2}(M;F)
it holds that
t\mapsto D_{t,\max}
defines a norm-continuous mapping
[0,1]\to\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{0,\max}),{\rm L}^{2}(M;F))
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is slightly technical and relies on the construction of approximate Calderón projectors following Theorem
3.8
and
3.18
. The family
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
extends to a continuous family of elliptic differential operators on a closed manifold containing
M
as a domain, this is as in the proof of Theorem
3.18
above. We follow the notation in the proof of Theorem
3.18
. Since the family
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is continuous in the topology on the space of order
m
differential operators, we can construct a continuous family
(\hat{T}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subseteq\Psi^{-m}_{\rm cl}(M;F,E)
such that
\hat{T}_{t}
is a parametrix of
\hat{D}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Following Equation (
6
), we define an approximate Poisson operator

\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}\left(\xi_{j}\right)_{j=0}^{m-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\sum_{l=0}^{m-1-j}(-i)^{l+1}\hat{T}_{t}[A_{m-l-j-1}\xi_{j}\otimes\delta_{t=0}^{(l)}].

Using the same arguments as in
[
64
]
, it holds that
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}:\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\to\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\rm max})
continuously for all
t\in[0,1]
. Indeed, letting
\mathcal{K}_{D_{t}}
denote the Poisson operator of
D_{t}
(see
[
64
]
or Theorem
3.18
) above, we have that
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}-\mathcal{K}_{D_{t}}:\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)
. We therefore also have that
\gamma\circ\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}-P_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a smoothing operator, and in particular
\gamma\circ\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}
is an approximate Calderón projection as in Theorem
3.8
.

Consider the family of bounded operators
R_{t}:=\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}\circ\gamma:\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\to\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})
. We note that for
f\in C^{\infty}(M;E)
,

D_{t}R_{t}f=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\sum_{l=0}^{m-1-j}(-i)^{l+1}(D_{t}\hat{T}_{t}-1)[A_{m-l-j-1}\gamma_{j}(f)\otimes\delta_{t=0}^{(l)}],

because distributions supported on
\Sigma
vanish when restricted to
M
. However, since
\hat{T}_{t}
is a continuous family of parametrices, the mapping
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto(\hat{D}_{t}\hat{T}_{t}-1)\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\hat{M};\hat{F})
is continuous. We conclude that
t\mapsto D_{t}R_{t}
factors over
\scalebox{1.5}{a}\gamma:\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max})\to\hat{\mathrm{H}}(D^{\dagger})
, the continuous inclusion
\hat{\mathrm{H}}(D^{\dagger})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma;F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and a continuous family of operators
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma;F\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
. As such,
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto D_{t}R_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max}),{\rm H}^{\rm s}(M;F))
is norm-continuous for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and in particular
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto D_{t}R_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{\max}),{\rm L}^{2}(M;F))
is norm-continuous.

Since
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}-\mathcal{K}_{D_{t}}:\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)
, we have that
(1-R_{t}):\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max})\to{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)
. Indeed, we can write
\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max})={\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)+\ker(D_{t,\max})
by Example
3.21
and
1-R_{t}
is smoothing on
\ker(D_{t,\max})
because of
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{t}-\mathcal{K}_{D_{t}}:\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)
. Using the semi-classical nature of the arguments in
[
64
]
, it follows that
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto 1-R_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max}),{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E))
is norm-continuous. It is clear that
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto D_{t}\in\mathbb{B}({\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E),{\rm L}^{2}(M,E))
is continuous. Therefore
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto D_{t}(1-R_{t})\in\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max}),{\rm L}^{2}(M,E))
.

Since the map
t\mapsto D_{t}=D_{t}R_{t}+D_{t}(1-R_{t})
is a sum of continuous maps
[0,1]\to\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{\rm max}),{\rm L}^{2}(M,E))
, the map
t\mapsto D_{t,\max}
defines a norm-continuous mapping
[0,1]\to\mathbb{B}(\mathrm{dom}(D_{0,\max}),{\rm L}^{2}(M;F))
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume that
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of elliptic differential operators of order
m>0
as in Theorem
8.1
and define the family of self-adjoint operators

\tilde{D}_{t}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&D_{t,{\rm min}}\\
D^{\dagger}_{t,{\rm max}}&0\end{pmatrix},

for
t\in[0,1]
. Then for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, the mapping
t\mapsto(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t})^{-1}
is a norm-continuous mapping
[0,1]\to\mathbb{B}({\rm L}^{2}(M;F\oplus E))
.

Proof.

By the assumptions of Theorem
8.1
, the domain of
\tilde{D}_{t}
is constant in
t
. We have for
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]
that

(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t_{1}})^{-1}-(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t_{2}})^{-1}=(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t_{1}})^{-1}(\tilde{D}_{t_{2}}-\tilde{D}_{t_{1}})(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t_{2}})^{-1}

Since
(\lambda i+\tilde{D}_{t})^{-1}:{\rm L}^{2}(M;F\oplus E)\to\mathrm{dom}(\tilde{D}_{t})=\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\min})\oplus\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\max}^{\dagger})
with the uniform norm bound
|\lambda|^{-1}
as
t
varies, the lemma follows from Lemma
8.2
(applied to
(D_{t}^{\dagger})_{t\in[0,1]}
) and the fact that
t\mapsto D_{t,\min}
clearly defines a norm-continuous mapping
[0,1]\to\mathbb{B}({\rm H}^{\rm m}_{\rm 0}(M;E),{\rm L}^{2}(M;F))
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

The ellipticity of each
D_{t}
ensures that
\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\min})={\rm H}^{\rm m}_{\rm 0}(M;E)
is constant in
t
. We remark that item
2
ensures that
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D_{t})\cong\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\max})/\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\min})=\mathrm{dom}(D_{t,\max})/{\rm H}^{\rm m}_{\rm 0}(M;E)
is independent of
t
, justifying the notation
\check{\mathrm{H}}(D)
for the Cauchy data space.

The only remaining property to verify in order to invoke Theorem
B.32
below is that the mappings

[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&D_{t,{\rm min}}\\
D^{\dagger}_{t,{\rm max}}&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&D^{\dagger}_{t,{\rm min}}\\
D_{t,{\rm max}}&0\end{pmatrix},

are continuous in the gap topology.
Lemma
8.3
applied to the families
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
and
(D_{t}^{\dagger})_{t\in[0,1]}
furnishes us with this fact.
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
D
be an elliptic differential operator and
B
a regular boundary condition. Assume that
B
is graphically decomposed with respect to the Calderon projector by
(V_{+},W_{+},V_{-},W_{-},g)
as in Definition
5.3
. Then it holds that

\indx(D_{B})=\dim(W_{+})-\dim(W_{-})+\dim(\ker(D_{\min})-\dim(\ker(D_{\min}^{\dagger}).

Proof.

We define the continuous path of regular boundary conditions
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
from the continuous path of data for a graphical decomposition
(V_{+},W_{+},V_{-},W_{-},tg)_{t\in[0,1]}
. Note that

B_{0}=W_{+}\oplus V_{-}\quad\mbox{and}\quad B_{0}^{*}=\scalebox{1.5}{a}_{\dagger}^{-1}({V}^{\ast}_{+}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}_{\rm op})\oplus{W}^{\ast}_{-}).

(26)

We also note that
V_{+}\oplus W_{+}=\mathcal{C}_{D}\cap{\mathbb{H}}^{m-1/2}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
by the definition of a graphical decomposition with respect to the Calderon projection. By Theorem
8.1
, we have that

\indx(D_{B})=\indx(D_{B_{0}})=\indx(D_{B_{1}}).

The proof now follows from Theorem
2.12
upon proving that
\indx(B_{0},\mathcal{C}_{D})=\dim(W_{+})-\dim(W_{-})
. This follows from the fact that
B_{0}\cap\mathcal{C}_{D}=W_{+}
and
B_{0}^{*}\cap\mathcal{C}_{D^{\dagger}}=\scalebox{1.5}{a}_{\dagger}^{-1}({W}^{\ast}_{-})
by Equation (
26
).
∎

Theorem 8.5
.

Assume that
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a family of elliptic differential operators of order
m>0
which is continuous in the topology on the space of order
m
differential operators
{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;E)\to{\rm C}^{\infty}(M;F)
. Let
(P_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subseteq\mathbb{B}({\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}))
be a norm continuous family of projectors such that

(1)

For all
t\in[0,1]
,
P_{t}
extends to a bounded operator on
{\mathbb{H}}^{{-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

(2)

For all
t\in[0,1]
,
\alpha\in\{-1/2,m-1/2\}
the operator

P_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}-(1-P_{t}):{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\to{\mathbb{H}}^{\alpha}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}),

(27)

is a Fredholm operator, where
P_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}
denotes the Calderon projector for
D_{t}
.

Then for all
t\in[0,1]
,
B_{t}:=(1-P_{t}){\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a regular boundary condition for
D_{t}
and the index
\indx(D_{t,B_{t}})
is independent of
t
. In particular,

\indx(D_{0,B_{0}})=\indx(D_{1,B_{1}}).

The proof goes along similar lines as that of Theorem
B.32
but adapted to regular realisations with domains contained in Sobolev spaces. We note that Theorem
8.5
in particular holds for a continuous family
(P_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subseteq\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
of pseudodifferential projectors such that for all
t\in[0,1]

p_{+}(D_{t})-(1-\sigma_{\boldsymbol{0}}(P_{t}))\in C^{\infty}(S^{*}\Sigma;\mathrm{Aut}(\pi^{*}E)).

Proof.

We note that each
B_{t}
is a regular boundary condition by Theorem
4.14
. Since
(D_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous family of elliptic differential operators, it defines a bounded adjointable map of
C[0,1]
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules

\mathbb{D}:{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)\otimes C[0,1]\to{\rm L}^{2}(M;F)\otimes C[0,1].

We write
\check{C}_{t}:=P_{t}{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
and let
\check{C}_{C([0,1])}
denote the
C[0,1]
-Hilbert
C^{*}
-submodule of
{\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})\otimes C([0,1])
with fibre
\check{C}_{t}
, it is well-defined by norm continuity of the family
(P_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
. We write
P\gamma:{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)\otimes C([0,1])\to\check{C}_{C([0,1])}
for the bounded adjointable mapping obtained from composing the trace mapping with the family
(P_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Following Proposition
B.30
and Theorem
B.32
, we define the operator

\check{L}:{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)\otimes C[0,1]\to\begin{matrix}{\rm L}^{2}(M;F)\otimes C[0,1]\\
\oplus\\
\check{C}\end{matrix},\quad\check{L}u=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{D}u\\
P\gamma u\end{pmatrix}

This operator is an adjointable
C[0,1]
-linear map because
\mathbb{D}
and
P\gamma
are adjointable. We note that
\check{L}
if Fredholm in each fibre by Proposition
B.30
(cf.
[
7
, Proposition A.1]
). By an argument along the same lines as in Theorem
B.32
, the adjointable
C[0,1]
-linear map
\check{L}:{\rm H}^{\rm m}(M;E)\otimes C[0,1]\to{\rm L}^{2}(M;F)\otimes C[0,1]\oplus\check{C}
is Fredholm with a well defined index
\indx(\check{L})\in K^{0}([0,1])
. Again arguing as in Theorem
B.32
, homotopy invariance of
K
-theory implies that

\indx(D_{t,B_{t}})=\indx(\check{L}_{t})=r_{t}^{*}\indx(\check{L}),

where
r_{t}
denotes the inclusion
\{t\}\subseteq[0,1]
, is independent of
t
.
∎

We end this section with an outlook towards index theory. It poses an interesting problem to compute
\indx(D_{{\rm B}_{P}})
where
D
is an elliptic differential operator
D
of order
m>0
and
B_{P}:=(1-P){\mathbb{H}}^{{m-{\frac{1}{2}}}}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a regular pseudo-local boundary condition on
D
defined from
P\in\Psi^{\boldsymbol{0}}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m})
. While Theorem
2.12
and Corollary
5.7
give formulas, a more worthwhile goal would be an explicit formula for the index of
D_{\rm B}
in terms of the interior principal symbol of
D
, the germ of
D
on
\Sigma
, the differential operator

a

defining the boundary pairing and the projection
P
. The Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem and the index theorem in the extended Boutet de Monvel calculus of
[
63
]
indicates that such an explicit computation is possible. Under some mild assumptions on
P
, the special case of first order elliptic operators is susceptible to heat kernel techniques by the results of
[
44
]
.

There is an interesting special case that the authors of this paper have not found an explicit solution to in the literature. A classical situation is that
\not{D}
is a Dirac operator and
P
is a spectral projection of the boundary operator of
\not{D}
constructed from a normal vector field to the boundary. Atiyah-Patodi-Singer
[
4
]
computed the index in the case of product structure at the boundary and
[
42
]
considered the general case. For more details, see also
[
15
,
59
]
. Assume that we leave this classical realm and instead use a general vector field transversal to the boundary to define the adapted boundary operator
A
. Then
A
is not necessarily self-adjoint, but only bisectorial (cf.
[
8
]
). Is there an explicit formula for the index
\indx(D_{{\rm B}_{P}})
for
P=\chi^{+}(A)
? Preferably, such a formula only uses local data and spectral data of the bisectorial operator
A
. The results of
[
44
]
indicates that there might be a positive answer. The importance of this question is seen in that similar questions can be asked on manifolds with Lipschitz boundary, where a smooth vector field transversal to the boundary (almost everywhere) makes sense.

9.
Symbol computations with Calderón projectors in the first order case

In this section, we study the way in which the Calderón projection of a first order elliptic operator relates to the the positive spectral projection of an adapted boundary operator. We show that the difference of these projectors are an operator of order
-1
and continuous, but in general not compact on the Cauchy data space.

Near the boundary,

D=\sigma\partial_{x_{n}}+A_{1},

(28)

where
A_{1}=(A_{1}(x_{n}))_{x_{n}\in[0,1]}
is a family of first order differential operators acting from
E|_{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
to
F|_{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
. By ellipticity of
D
,
\sigma
is a bundle isomorphism and
A_{1}
is a family of elliptic differential operators. We note that an adapted boundary operator for
D
is precisely a differential operator
A
on
E|_{\Sigma}
such that
A-\sigma^{-1}A_{1}|_{x_{n}=0}
is of order zero.

Recall the construction of
E_{+}(D)
and
p_{+}(D)
from the subsection
3.1
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
D
be a first order elliptic differential operator and
A
an adapted boundary operator on
E|_{\Sigma}
. Then
\chi^{+}(A)
is a classical order zero pseudo-differential operator whose principal symbol is
p_{+}(D)
.

Proof.

Since
\chi^{+}(A)
is constructed as a sectorial projection, it is a classical order zero pseudo-differential operator, see
[
47
]
. Its principal symbol is the same as
\chi^{+}(a)
where
a
is the principal symbol of
A
. By construction,
a=\sigma^{-1}a_{1}
where
a_{1}
is the principal symbol of
A_{1}|_{x_{n}=0}
appearing in the collar decomposition
D=\sigma\partial_{x_{n}}+A_{1}
. We conclude that
\sigma_{\partial}(D)=\sigma\partial_{x_{n}}+a_{1}=\sigma(\partial_{x_{n}}+a)
. It is clear that for
(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})\in S^{*}\Sigma
and
v_{0}\in E_{x}
, Equation (
5
), that in this case takes the form

\begin{cases}\sigma(\partial_{t}+a(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))v(t)=0,\;t>0,\\
v(0)=v_{0},\end{cases}

has an exponentially decaying solution if and only if
v_{0}\in\chi^{+}(a(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))E_{x}
, namely
v(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-a(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})t}v_{0}
. Similarly, there is an exponentially increasing solution if and only if
v_{0}\in\chi^{-}(a(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))E_{x}
. So
E_{+}(D)=\chi^{+}(a)E
and
E_{-}(D)=\chi^{-}(a)E
and we can conclude that the principal symbol of
\chi^{+}(A)
equals
p_{+}(D)
.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

By Proposition
3.4
, we know that
E_{+}(D)\neq 0
and
E_{-}(D)\neq 0
. In fact, each fibre is non-zero if the dimension exceeds
1
. For first order operators, this can be proven more directly using the symmetry
a(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=-a(x^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime})
which implies that
p_{+}(D)(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=1-p_{+}(D)(x^{\prime},-\xi^{\prime})
so
p_{+}(D)\neq 0
and a similar argument implies
p_{-}(D)\neq 0
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
D
be a Dirac type operator and
A
an adapted boundary operator on
E|_{\Sigma}
such that

D=\sigma(\partial_{x_{n}}+A),

near the boundary. Then
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\Sigma;E)
is a smoothing operator.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\chi^{+}(A)-Q\in\Psi^{-\infty}(\Sigma;E)
is a smoothing operator for
Q
being the approximate Calderón projection from Theorem
3.8
. Since the construction in Theorem
3.8
is microlocal at the boundary we can assume that
M=\Sigma\times[0,\infty)
. In this case, any
f\in\ker D_{\rm max}
can be written in the form

f(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-tA}f(0).

As such,
\mathcal{K}g(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-tA}\chi^{+}(A)g
and the Calderón projection
P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}=\chi^{+}(A)
is an approximate Calderón projection.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
D
be a first order elliptic differential operator with adapted boundary operator
A
. The first term in the Calderón projection is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}(P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})=

\displaystyle p_{+}(D)=\chi^{+}(\sigma_{1}(A)).

In coordinates near a point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{-1}(P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\sum_{z:\mathrm{Re}(z)>0}\mathrm{Res}_{z=\lambda}t_{1}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},i\lambda),

where

\displaystyle t_{1}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},i\lambda)=

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}(c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})+c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},0))(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}-

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}c_{A}\left(x^{\prime},(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{x^{\prime}}c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\right)-

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-2}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}.

Here, we have written
D=\sigma(\partial_{x_{n}}+A+R_{0})
for an
x_{n}
-dependent first order differential operator
R_{0}
on
\Sigma
,
c_{A}
denotes the principal symbol of
A
,
c_{A,0}
the sub-leading term (and similarly for
A
), and where we use the notation
c_{A}(x^{\prime},\nu\otimes M):=c_{A}(x^{\prime},\nu)M
for an endomorphism valued covector
\nu\otimes M
.

Proof.

By the formula for the full symbol of the approximate Calderón projection from Theorem
3.8
, we have that

Qf=\gamma_{0}\circ T_{0}[\sigma f\otimes\delta_{t=0}],

where
T_{0}
is a parametrix for
D
. In particular, we note that

Qf=\gamma_{0}\circ T[f\otimes\delta_{t=0}],

where
T
is a parametrix for
\sigma^{-1}D
. The operator
T
is a classical pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
in a neighbourhood of
M
in an extended manifold. In coordinates near a point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
, a full symbol
t
of
T
has an asymptotic expansion
t\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}t_{j}
and each
t_{j}=t_{j}(x,\xi)
is smooth in
x
and homogeneous of degree
-1-j
in
\xi
. Using the methodology of
[
50
, Chapter XX]
, cf. Remark
3.9
, the full symbol of
Q
can be computed by

q_{j}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\sum_{z:\mathrm{Im}(z)>0}\mathrm{Res}_{\xi_{n}=z}t_{j}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n}),

where
(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
denote the associated coordinates on
\Sigma
. As such, we shall need to compute the two terms in the asymptotic expansion of
T
. By an abuse of notation, we will write
t_{j}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})
for
t_{j}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})
.

To compute the asymptotic expansion of
T
, we write the full symbol of
D
as
c_{D}+c_{D,0}
where
c_{D}
is the principal symbol (i.e. a homogeneous degree 1 polynomial) and
c_{D,0}
is
\xi
-independent. We can write
D=\sigma(D_{t}+A+R_{0})
for an
x_{n}
-dependent first order differential operator
R_{0}
on
\Sigma
with
R_{0}|_{x_{n}=0}
being a multiplication operator. We use similar notation for
A
and
R_{0}
and note that

\begin{cases}c_{D}(x^{\prime},x_{n},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})&=\sigma(x^{\prime},x_{n})(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})+c_{R_{0}}(x^{\prime},x_{n},\xi^{\prime})),\;\mbox{and}\\
c_{D,0}(x^{\prime},x_{n})&=\sigma(x^{\prime},x_{n})(c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})+c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},x_{n})).\end{cases}

The assumption that
A
is adapted ensures that
c_{R_{0}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})=0
. To compute the parametrix of
\sigma^{-1}D
, we use the symbols

\begin{cases}\tilde{c}_{D}(x^{\prime},x_{n},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})&:=\sigma(x^{\prime},x_{n})^{-1}c_{D}(x^{\prime},x_{n},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})=i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})+c_{R_{0}}(x^{\prime},x_{n},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n}),\\
\tilde{c}_{D,0}(x^{\prime},x_{n})&:=\sigma(x^{\prime},x_{n})^{-1}c_{D,0}(x^{\prime},x_{n})=c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})+c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},x_{n}).\end{cases}

From the well known parametrix construction, the full symbol
t\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}t_{j}
of
T
is computed inductively from that
t_{0}(x,\xi)=c_{D}(x,\xi)^{-1}
and

\displaystyle t_{j+1}=

\displaystyle-t_{0}\tilde{c}_{D,0}t_{j}-t_{0}\sum_{k+|\alpha|=j+1,\,k\leq j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\tilde{c}_{D}D^{\alpha}_{x}t_{k}.

In particular,

\displaystyle t_{1}=

\displaystyle-t_{0}\tilde{c}_{D,0}t_{0}-t_{0}\sum_{|\alpha|=1}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\tilde{c}_{D}D^{\alpha}_{x}t_{0}.

At
x_{n}=0
, we have that

\displaystyle t_{0}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})=

\displaystyle(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1},

and

\displaystyle t_{1}

\displaystyle(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\xi_{n})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}(c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})+c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},0))(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}+

\displaystyle+(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}c_{A}(x^{\prime},\mathrm{d}x_{j})(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\frac{\partial c_{A}}{\partial x_{j}}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}+

\displaystyle+(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\tilde{c}_{D}((x^{\prime},0),\mathrm{d}x_{n})(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}(c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})+c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},0))(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}+

\displaystyle+(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}c_{A}\left(x^{\prime},(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{x^{\prime}}c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\right)+

\displaystyle+(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-2}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})(i\xi_{n}+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}.\qed

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
A
be an elliptic first order differential operator on
\Sigma
. The first term in the positive spectral projection is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}(\chi^{+}(A))=

\displaystyle\chi^{+}(\sigma_{1}(A)).

In coordinates near a point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A))=

\displaystyle\sum_{z:\mathrm{Re}(z)>0}\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=z}b_{1}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda),

where in the notations of Lemma
9.4

\displaystyle b_{1}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}c_{A,0}(x^{\prime})(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}+

(29)

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}c_{A}\left(x^{\prime},(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{x^{\prime}}c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\right).

Proof.

Consider
B:=(\lambda-A)^{-1}
as a pseudodifferential with a parameter. Picking coordinates near a point
x_{0}\in\Sigma
, we write the full symbol with parameter of
B
as
b\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}b_{j}
, where
b_{j}=b_{j}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)
is smooth in
x^{\prime}
and homogeneous of degree
-1-j
in
(\xi^{\prime},\lambda)
. By
[
47
, Theorem 3.3]
, the full symbol of
\chi^{+}(A)
has the asymptotic expansion
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\chi_{j}
where

\chi_{j}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=\sum_{z:\mathrm{Re}(z)>0}\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=z}b_{j}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda).

As such, we shall need to compute the first two terms in the asymptotic expansion of
B
.

Using the notations of Lemma
9.4
and its proof, we can compute
b_{j}
inductively from that
b_{0}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}
and

\displaystyle b_{j+1}=

\displaystyle-b_{0}c_{A,0}b_{j}-b_{0}\sum_{k+|\alpha|=j+1,\,k\leq j}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}c_{A}D^{\alpha}_{x}b_{k}.

In particular,

\displaystyle b_{1}=

\displaystyle-b_{0}c_{A,0}b_{0}-b_{0}\sum_{|\alpha|=1}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}c_{A}D^{\alpha}_{x}b_{0},

which is readily computed to reproduce the formula in Equation (
29
).
∎

Definition 9.6
.

Let
D
be an elliptic first order differential operator with adapted boundary operator
A
. We say that
A
is a well-adapted boundary operator if
A=\sigma^{-1}A_{1}|_{x_{n}=0}
, where
A_{1}
is as in Equation (
28
).

We note that upon fixing a choice of normal to the boundary, there is a uniquely determined well adapted boundary operator. In the notations of Lemma
9.4
,
A
is well adapted if and only if
c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},0)=0
which holds if and only if
R_{0}|_{x_{n}=0}=0
. The following Proposition is immediate from Lemma
9.4
and
9.5
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
D
be a first order elliptic differential operator with adapted boundary operator
A
. Then
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
is of order
-1
and its principal symbol is in the notation of Lemma
9.4
given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})=

\displaystyle\sum_{z:\mathrm{Re}(z)>0}\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=z}p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda),

where

\displaystyle p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}c_{R_{0},0}(x^{\prime},0)(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}+

\displaystyle+(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-2}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1},

If
A
is well adapted, then
\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})
is constructed from residues of the simpler symbol

\displaystyle p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-2}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime})(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}.

Corollary 9.8
.

Let
D
be a first order elliptic differential operator with well adapted boundary operator
A
.

•

If
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
commutes with
c_{A}
, then
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}\in\Psi^{-2}_{\rm cl}(M;E)
and acts compactly on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
.

•

If
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
anti-commutes with
c_{A}
, then

\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})=\frac{1}{4}c_{A}^{-2}\chi^{-}(c_{A})\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}.

Proof.

The first item follows from Proposition
9.7
if we can prove that
\mathrm{Res}_{\lambda=z}p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=0
for all
z
. Since
A
is well adapted, and
c_{A}
commutes with
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
, Proposition
9.7
ensures that

\displaystyle p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-3}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime}).

In particular, the residues of
p
vanish if
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
commutes with
c_{A}
.

On the other hand, if
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
anti-commutes with
c_{A}

\displaystyle p(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\lambda)=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-2}(\lambda+c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))^{-1}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime}).

Using Proposition
9.7
we compute that in this case

\displaystyle\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})^{-2}\chi^{-}(c_{A}(x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}))\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}(x^{\prime},0,\xi^{\prime}).\qed

Proposition 9.9
.

Let
D
be an elliptic first order differential operator with adapted boundary operator
A
. Then
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
is compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if

\sigma_{0}(\chi^{-}(A))\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\sigma_{0}(\chi^{+}(A))=0,

as elements of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(S^{*}\Sigma;\mathrm{End}(\pi^{*}E))
.

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}=

\displaystyle\chi^{+}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(A)+\chi^{-}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{-}(A)+

\displaystyle\qquad+\chi^{+}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{-}(A)+\chi^{-}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi^{+}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(A)+\chi^{-}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{-}(A)+

\displaystyle\quad+\chi^{+}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{-}(A)+\chi^{-}(A)(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(A)

Since
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}\in\Psi^{-1}_{\rm cl}(\Sigma;E)
the first two terms are compact for any adapted boundary operator
A
. The third term is a pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
and it acts compactly on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if it acts compactly
{\rm H}^{\rm{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E)\to{\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E)
and as such the third term is compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
for any adapted boundary operator
A
. The fourth term is a pseudodifferential operator of order
-1
and it acts compactly on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if it acts compactly
{\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E)\to{\rm H}^{\rm{\frac{1}{2}}}(\Sigma;E)
and as such the fourth term is compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if its symbol vanish, i.e.
\sigma_{0}(\chi^{-}(A))\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\sigma_{0}(\chi^{+}(A))=0
.
∎

Proposition 9.10
.

Let
D
be a first order elliptic differential operator with well adapted boundary operator
A
and assume that
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
anti-commutes with
c_{A}
. Then
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
is compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if

\chi^{-}(c_{A})\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}=0.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.9
, it suffices to prove that
\chi^{-}(c_{A})\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(c_{A})=0
if and only if
\chi^{-}(c_{A})\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}=0
when
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
anti-commutes with
c_{A}
. But by Corollary
9.8
,

\displaystyle\chi^{-}(c_{A})\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(c_{A})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}c_{A}^{-2}\chi^{-}(c_{A})\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}\chi^{+}(c_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}c_{A}^{-2}\chi^{-}(c_{A})\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma},

where we in the last line used that
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
anti-commutes with
c_{A}
. The proposition follows from that
c_{A}
is invertible.
∎

Example 9.11
.

Let us give an easy example showcasing when
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
fails to be compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
. Take
M
to be the unit disc and
E=F=M\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

We first consider the rather straight forward example of the Dirac type operator
D_{0}
given in polar coordinates by

D_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}\\
-\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}&0\end{pmatrix}=\sigma(\partial_{r}+A+R_{00}),

where

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad A=\begin{pmatrix}-i\partial_{\theta}&0\\
0&i\partial_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{00}=(r^{-1}-1)A.

It is readily verified that
A
is a well adapted boundary operator, and in this case it is self-adjoint. A short computation with the Cauchy-Riemann equation in polar coordinates show that
P_{\mathcal{C}_{D_{0}}}=\chi^{+}(A)
for the operator
D_{0}
.

To jazz up this example a bit, we define

R_{0}:=R_{00}-i\alpha\sigma\partial_{\theta},

where
\alpha=\alpha(r)
is a smooth function
\alpha\in{\rm C}^{\infty}_{\rm c}(0,1]
with
\alpha(1)=0
. We define

D_{\alpha}:=\sigma(\partial_{r}+A+R_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}i\alpha\partial_{\theta}&\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}\\
-\partial_{r}+\frac{i}{r}\partial_{\theta}&i\alpha\partial_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}.

The reader should note that
D_{\alpha}
is formally self-adjoint if and only if
\alpha
is real valued and
A
is well adapted for any choice of
\alpha
. Let us compute
\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})
in this case. For simplicity, we write
\xi
for the cotangent variable on
\Sigma=S^{1}
. We have that

c_{A}(\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}-\xi&0\\
0&\xi\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{and}\quad(\lambda-c_{A})^{-1}(\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}(\lambda+\xi)^{-1}&0\\
0&(\lambda-\xi)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

We have
c_{R_{0}}(r,\xi)=(r^{-1}-1)c_{A}-\alpha\sigma\xi
and compute that

\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial r}|_{\Sigma}(\xi)=-c_{A}(\xi)-\alpha^{\prime}(1)\sigma\xi.

Although Corollary
9.8
does not give an immediate answer because
\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial r}|_{\Sigma}
does not commute nor anticommute with
c_{A}
in this case, we have a sum of a commuting and anticommuting term and a short computation gives us that

\displaystyle p(\xi,\lambda)=

\displaystyle-(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-2}\frac{\partial c_{R_{0}}}{\partial r}(r,\xi)|_{r=1}(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-2}c_{A}(\xi)(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-1}+(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-2}\alpha^{\prime}(1)\sigma\xi(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-3}c_{A}(\xi)+\alpha^{\prime}(1)\xi(\lambda-c_{A}(\xi))^{-2}(\lambda+c_{A}(\xi))^{-1}\sigma.

The first term has no residues, and the residues of the second term is computed as in Corollary
9.8
to produce

\displaystyle\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})(\xi)=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)\xi}{4}c_{A}^{-2}\chi^{-}(c_{A})\sigma=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4\xi}\chi^{-}(c_{A})\sigma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)}{\xi}\\
-\frac{\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)}{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix},

using that

\chi^{-}(c_{A})=\sigma_{0}(\chi^{-}(A))=\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)&0\\
0&\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)\end{pmatrix}.

We see that
\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\neq 0
if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}(1)\neq 0
. Moreover, since

\displaystyle\chi^{-}(c_{A})\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\chi^{+}(c_{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)}{\xi}\\
-\frac{\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)}{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)&0\\
0&\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)}{\xi}\\
-\frac{\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)}{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix},

we see that
\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
fails to be compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
if and only if
\alpha^{\prime}(1)\neq 0
by Proposition
9.9
. Let us summarise the salient features of this example in a proposition.

Proposition 9.12
.

Assume that
M
is the unit disc,
E=F=M\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
and take
D_{\alpha}
as in Example
9.11
for an
\alpha
with
\alpha^{\prime}(1)\neq 0
. We let
A
denote its well adapted boundary operator. Then it holds that

(1)

\sigma_{-1}(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})\neq 0
.

(2)

\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
fails to be compact on
\check{\mathrm{H}}_{A}(D)
.

(3)

The space
\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
is a finite-dimensional subspace of
{\rm C}^{\infty}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proof.

The first two statements were already proven in Example
9.11
. It remains to prove that last.

We define the operator
\Lambda:=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}(1+\Delta)^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}\in\Psi^{-1}_{\rm cl}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Let us first prove the claim that
L:=\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}+\sigma\Lambda\chi^{-}(A)\in\Psi^{-1}_{\rm cl}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is elliptic. We have that

\sigma_{-1}(L)=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)}{\xi}-\frac{\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)}{\xi}\\
-\frac{\chi_{(-\infty,0)}(\xi)}{\xi}-\frac{\chi_{(0,\infty)}(\xi)}{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(1)}{4}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{|\xi|}\\
-\frac{1}{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix}.

This is invertible for all
\xi\neq 0
, so
L
is elliptic.

Consider the space
\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap{\mathcal{C}_{D}}
. We have the following equivalence

\displaystyle f\in\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap{\mathcal{C}_{D}}

\displaystyle\iff\begin{cases}\chi^{+}(A)f=f\\
P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}}f=f\end{cases}

\displaystyle\iff\begin{cases}\chi^{+}(A)f=f\\
(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})f=0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\iff\begin{cases}\chi^{+}(A)f=f\\
Lf=0.\end{cases}

In the last equivalence we used that if
\chi^{+}(A)f=f
, then
\chi^{-}(A)f=0
and
\chi^{-}(A)f=0
implies that
(\chi^{+}(A)-P_{\mathcal{C}_{D}})f=Lf
. We can conclude that
\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap{\mathcal{C}_{D}}=\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-{\frac{1}{2}}}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})\cap\ker(L)
. But since
L
is elliptic,
\ker(L)\subseteq{\rm C}^{\infty}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is a finite-dimensional subspace and item
3
follows.
∎

Although Proposition
9.12
shows that
\ker(D_{\chi^{+}(A){\rm H}^{\rm-\frac{1}{2}}})
